[Director 指令] [Patch] 用户反馈论文正文太空，需要更多图表充实每页。以下是经过仔细筛选的布局升级方案。

**核心原则**：
- 除 P1 外，每页顶部尽量填满双栏（左栏顶 + 右栏顶都有 float）
- 所有 float 用 `[t]` 或 `[t!]` 强制顶部
- 提升的内容必须对叙事有实质帮助
- 主文 ≤ 8 页

**变更 1：Figure 1 改为 figure* 全宽**
当前 Figure 1（pipeline overview）太小。改用 `\begin{figure*}[t]`，`width=\textwidth`。Pipeline 概念图全宽是 ML 论文标准做法。这会让 P2 顶部视觉非常充实。

**变更 2：提升 3 个附录内容到正文**

(a) **Algorithm 1**（alg:labeling）→ 移入 method.tex §3.3 Episode-Level Labeling 段落处
- 之前试过导致溢出。这次配合文本压缩一起做
- 算法本身约 20 行，占 ~0.35 页
- 在 P3 或 P4 与 Table 1 或 Figure 2 搭配

(b) **tab:per-task**（11 task × 6 cols 核心分解表）→ 移入 experiments.tex §4.1
- 这是论文最核心的结果表，展示每个 task 的 death rate、label count、Δ_full
- 直接支撑 Figure 3（scatter plot），读者可以对照看
- 与 Figure 3 同页或相邻页，形成双栏 float

(c) **tab:cross-model-env**（5 models × 3 environments 汇总表）→ 移入 §4.2 或 §5 Discussion
- 一张表展示全部跨模型跨环境验证结果
- 替代正文中大段散述的数字，让文字更精炼

**变更 3：文本压缩（为新 float 腾空间）**

需要压缩约 1-1.5 页文本来容纳新增 floats。压缩优先级：
1. **Related Work**（~1.5 页 → ~1 页）：每个子节保留核心引用 + 1 句定位，删除详细方法描述
2. **Experiments 段落**：将行内数字移入表格后，对应的文字描述可以大幅精简（"如 Table X 所示"替代逐个列举数字）
3. **Discussion**：压缩冗余表述，重复性结论只保留一处
4. **Method**：公式推导保持不变，但文字说明可以收紧

**目标 layout（理想状态）**：

| Page | 左栏顶 | 右栏顶 | 内容 |
|------|--------|--------|------|
| P1 | — | — | Title + Abstract + §1 Intro |
| P2 | Figure 1* (全宽) | ← | §1 Intro 续 + §2 RW |
| P3 | Figure 2 | Algorithm 1 | §2 RW 续 + §3 Method |
| P4 | tab:per-task | Table 1 | §3 Method 续 + §4 Experiments |
| P5 | Figure 3 | (text) | §4.1 Decomposition |
| P6 | Figure 4 | tab:cross-model-env | §4.2 Multi-Model + §4.3 |
| P7 | Figure 5 | Table 2 | §4.3 续 + §4.4 Cross-Env |
| P8 | Figure 6 | (text or small table) | §4 续 + §5 Discussion + §6 Conclusion |

注意：这是理想目标，实际 float reflow 可能导致偏差。每步编译验证，必要时微调。

**执行顺序**：
1. 先做 Figure 1 → figure*，编译验证
2. 压缩 Related Work + Experiments + Discussion 文本
3. 逐个提升 Algorithm 1、tab:per-task、tab:cross-model-env
4. 每步编译检查页数，如有溢出立即进一步压缩
5. 最终验证全部 P2-P8 每页至少 1 个 float

请开始执行。如遇到 8 页装不下的情况，优先保留 tab:per-task（最核心），其次 tab:cross-model-env，最后 Algorithm 1。

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing preferences in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions, capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); removing death episodes reveals that oracle access provides almost no non-death advantage ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 9.6\% of total gap; \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}). Two independent 70B-class models yield DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$ (Llama-70B exceeds 100\%: death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap), and Reflexion ($N{=}3$) similarly drives DAF above 100\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics, and provides build-vs-buy signal from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~\textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} in ScienceWorld stem from death asymmetry---replicated across three 7--9B model families (DAF $88$--$90\%$), four interventions, $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$), and cross-environment probes (MATH, ALFWorld)---while a step-level router recovers only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	\begin{figure}[t]
18	\centering
19	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}
20	\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}.}
21	\label{fig:pipeline}
22	\end{figure}
23	
24	
25	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KojoeE9yNGJQ1WtyRfVUYm`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	Self-supervised methods learn tool invocation from loss signals \citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, token-level methods embed tools in the vocabulary \citep{toolkengpt-2023}, task-level decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules \citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based methods learn routing from reward \citep{retool-2025}.
6	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
7	Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
8	
9	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
10	Adaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \citep{graves2016act}. Mixture-of-Recursions \citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths in the LLM era; concurrent work further diversifies routing granularity: CogRouter \citep{cogrouter-2026} adapts per-step depth, ARES \citep{ares-2026} selects effort levels, and MTRouter \citep{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn settings; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
11	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models; cost-optimization routing (FrugalGPT~\citep{frugalgpt-2023}; AutoMix~\citep{automix-2024}) cascades queries across model tiers. These methods optimize \emph{which} model to invoke; our framework diagnoses \emph{what} drives the routing decision.
12	Concurrently, \citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing policies under sparse terminal rewards, the closest setting to ours.
13	Unlike token-level adaptive computation (Mixture-of-Depths~\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024}; early exit~\citep{xin2020deebert}) and Mixture-of-Experts~\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode-level granularity. Counterfactual data augmentation~\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to flip labels; we execute full counterfactual \emph{episodes}.
14	
15	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in Reinforcement Learning (RL).}
16	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent and concurrent extensions target LLM agents~\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \emph{before} training.
17	
18	\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}
19	ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. The concurrent SALT~\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.
20	
21	\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}
22	Death avoidance connects to constrained Markov decision processes (MDPs; \citealp{altman1999constrained}), risk-sensitive RL \citep{mihatsch2002risk}, safe RL \citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, and shielding \citep{alshiekh2018safe}; Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) methods~\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing} optimize against worst-case outcomes; our framework instead diagnoses which signals derive from catastrophic risk. Classical methods assume known dynamics or differentiable constraints, unavailable for LLM agents. \citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions as a failure category; SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench \citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address model selection and systematic evaluation for agent safety. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified instance of the potential outcomes framework~\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\citep{pearl2009causality}. \citet{bonagiri2025check} show selective quitting yields safety gains; our framework explains \emph{why}---death avoidance dominates routing signal.
```

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\nSelf-supervised methods learn tool invocation from loss signals \\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, token-level methods embed tools in the vocabulary \\citep{toolkengpt-2023}, task-level decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules \\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based methods learn routing from reward \\citep{retool-2025}.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nConcurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. Mixture-of-Recursions \\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths in the LLM era; concurrent work further diversifies routing granularity: CogRouter \\citep{cogrouter-2026} adapts per-step depth, ARES \\citep{ares-2026} selects effort levels, and MTRouter \\citep{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn settings; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models; cost-optimization routing (FrugalGPT~\\citep{frugalgpt-2023}; AutoMix~\\citep{automix-2024}) cascades queries across model tiers. These methods optimize \\emph{which} model to invoke; our framework diagnoses \\emph{what} drives the routing decision.\nConcurrently, \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing policies under sparse terminal rewards, the closest setting to ours.\nUnlike token-level adaptive computation (Mixture-of-Depths~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024}; early exit~\\citep{xin2020deebert}) and Mixture-of-Experts~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode-level granularity. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to flip labels; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes}.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in Reinforcement Learning (RL).}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent and concurrent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \\citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \\citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \\citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. The concurrent SALT~\\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}\nDeath avoidance connects to constrained Markov decision processes (MDPs; \\citealp{altman1999constrained}), risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk}, safe RL \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, and shielding \\citep{alshiekh2018safe}; Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing} optimize against worst-case outcomes; our framework instead diagnoses which signals derive from catastrophic risk. ```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property, but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants converge on near-chance accuracy ($56$--$59\%$). Two a priori unpredictable results: $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models fails to eliminate death dominance (DAF${}=92.4$--$101.0\%$; Llama-70B exceeds 100\%), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\%$; cross-model DR stability ($36$--$49\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property.
6	
7	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and practical implications.}
8	Always-deterministic matches 96.3\% of oracle decisions (\S\ref{sec:baselines}), capping routing's marginal value at 2.89 points. $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\% of total gap) shows the advantage concentrates almost entirely in death episodes; ALFWorld (DAF${=}0\%$) confirms the framework diagnoses qualitatively different mechanisms. A ${\sim}$50-episode pilot suffices for build-vs-buy decisions (Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The decomposition requires binary catastrophic failures, asymmetric failure rates, and $P \gg \Delta_\text{nd}$---ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH and ALFWorld lack the first.
9	
10	
```

```text
85	\end{equation}
86	where $n_k$ is the number of episodes and $\delta_k = d_k^{\tt int} - d_k^{\tt det}$ is the per-task death-rate differential. The SCI is the Gini coefficient over $\{c_1, \ldots, c_K\}$:
87	\begin{equation}
88	    \text{SCI} = \frac{\sum_{i=1}^K \sum_{j=1}^K |c_i - c_j|}{2K \sum_{k=1}^K c_k}
89	\end{equation}
90	
91	\section{Labeling Quality Audit}
92	\label{app:labeling}
93	
94	\begin{algorithm}[t]
95	\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}
96	\label{alg:labeling}
97	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
98	\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$
99	\ENSURE Label $\ell \in \{{\tt det\text{-}pref}, {\tt int\text{-}pref}, {\tt no\text{-}pref}\}$
100	\FOR{each $m \in \mathcal{M}$}
101	    \STATE Run episode $\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$
102	    \STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$
103	\ENDFOR
104	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
105	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
106	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
107	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
108	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
109	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
110	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
111	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
112	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
113	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
114	\ELSE
115	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
116	\ENDIF
117	\RETURN $\ell$
118	\end{algorithmic}
119	\smallskip
120	\noindent\textit{Assumption:} The death-first priority (lines 5--10) is valid when $P$ exceeds the maximum partial score, guaranteeing that a dead episode never outscores a surviving one. ScienceWorld satisfies this ($P{=}100$, max partial score ${=}100$).\\
121	\noindent\textit{Note:} When both strategies die (line 9), we assign \texttt{no\_preference}; in ScienceWorld, death yields a fixed $-100$ regardless of timing, so both-dead episodes contribute no routing signal.
122	\end{algorithm}
123	
124	Note that episode-level labels are used to evaluate step-level predictions, introducing a granularity mismatch rather than independent validation. A potential concern is oracle circularity: whether episode-level labels reflect tie-breaking artifacts rather than genuine computation mode differences. We audit all 245 labels. Of the 150 \texttt{no\_preference} labels (61.2\%), 115 (46.9\%) are both-alive ties and 35 (14.3\%) are both-dead, all excluded from router training. The remaining 95 training labels are entirely genuine: 75 from death asymmetry (70 deterministic-preferred, 5 internal-preferred) and 20 from score comparison with both strategies surviving (16 deterministic-preferred, 4 internal-preferred). Zero labels depend on tie-breaking. Retraining the router using only death-asymmetry labels yields step-level balanced accuracy of 57.3\%, within 1$\sigma$ of the full-label retrain (57.6 $\pm$ 0.5\%, step-level Trainer evaluation); score-comparison labels contribute negligible additional signal. (Step-level metrics are used here for the relative comparison; as shown in \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}, absolute generalization to new episodes is near chance.) The 97.4\% step-level tie rate (\S\ref{sec:collection}) further motivates episode-level over step-level labeling.
125	
126	\section{Per-Task Decomposition}
127	\label{app:per-task}
128	We use ScienceWorld v1.2.3~\citep{scienceworld-2022}.
129	We select 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types for analysis. Types are excluded when they were used for pipeline development (5 tasks, standard hold-out practice), are taxonomically redundant with a retained family representative (7 tasks), or exhibit near-zero task progress under both strategies (7 tasks, agent capability floor).\footnote{Excluded tasks exhibit even stronger death dominance than included tasks ($\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp vs.\ $+28.7$\,pp; Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}).}
130	
131	\begin{table}[h]
132	\centering
133	\scriptsize
134	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{2.5pt}
135	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrrccc@{}}
136	\toprule
137	Task & $n$ & $\Delta_\text{full}$ & D\%\textsubscript{int} & D\%\textsubscript{det} & D / I / N \\
138	\midrule
139	id-life-stages-1       & 20  & $+47.50$ & 55   & 10   & 9 / 0 / 11  \\
140	lifespan-long          & 25  & $+44.00$ & 56   & 16   & 11 / 1 / 13 \\
141	lifespan-l-then-s      & 20  & $+40.80$ & 45   & 10   & 8 / 1 / 11  \\
142	inclined-plane         & 20  & $+36.00$ & 55   & 20   & 7 / 0 / 13  \\
143	freeze                 & 20  & $+30.50$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\
144	meas-melt-point        & 20  & $+30.05$ & 55   & 25   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\
145	id-life-stages-2       & 20  & $+30.00$ & 50   & 20   & 6 / 0 / 14  \\
146	find-animal            & 20  & $+26.65$ & 50   & 25   & 5 / 0 / 15  \\
147	grow-fruit             & 20  & $+25.10$ & 45   & 20   & 6 / 1 / 13  \\
148	chem-mix               & 20  & $+21.80$ & 45   & 25   & 6 / 2 / 12  \\
149	\midrule
150	find-non-living-thing$^\dagger$ & 40 & $+9.58$ & 0  & 0    & 16 / 4 / 20 \\
151	\midrule
152	\textbf{All (11 tasks)} & \textbf{245} & $\mathbf{+29.60}$ & \textbf{42.9} & \textbf{16.3} & \textbf{86 / 9 / 150} \\
153	\bottomrule
154	\end{tabular}
155	\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. The \textit{All} row is an episode-weighted aggregate across 245 episodes. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
156	\label{tab:per-task}
157	\end{table}
158	
159	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
160	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
161

164	\scriptsize

169	Task & Grp & $n$ & D\%\textsubscript{int} & D\%\textsubscript{det} & $\Delta$DR & Excl.\ reason \\
170	\midrule

172	change-state-of-matter        & A & 20 & 55 & 15 & $+40$ & dev set \\
173	chem-mix-paint-secondary      & A & 20 & 45 & 10 & $+35$ & dev set \\
174	grow-plant                    & A & 20 &  0 &  0 & $\phantom{+}0$  & dev set \\

614	\caption{Cross-model and cross-environment replication summary. \textbf{ScienceWorld}: death dominance replicates across three model families in the 7--9B range and intensifies under $10{\times}$ scaling (two 70B-class models, DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$); DAF 95\% CIs from non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textbf{ALFWorld}: DAF $= 0\%$ (no death mechanism); routing signal arises from information asymmetry. \textbf{MATH}: no routing signal without catastrophic failure. Signal column: diagnosed routing mechanism. DR = death rate. Gemma-2's wider CI reflects one zero-death task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}); excluding it yields DAF $= 99.9\%$ $[99.7, 100.0]$.}
615	\label{tab:cross-model-env}
616	\end{table}
617	
618	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
619	\label{app:failure-modes}
620	
621	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
622	
623	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions. This taxonomy was produced by a single annotator; no inter-annotator agreement was computed. The categories are intended as qualitative illustrations, not as formal labels with reliability guarantees.
624	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
625	
626	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
627	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
628	
629	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
630	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy; both are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions, with \texttt{focus on} moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Crucially, Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} reveals that within death episodes, internal and deterministic strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$). The survival gap (105 vs.\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.
631	
632	\begin{figure}[h]
633	\centering
634	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}

  "old_string": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\nSelf-supervised methods learn tool invocation from loss signals \\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, token-level methods embed tools in the vocabulary \\citep{toolkengpt-2023}, task-level decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules \\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based methods learn routing from reward \\citep{retool-2025}.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nConcurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. Mixture-of-Recursions \\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths in the LLM era; concurrent work further diversifies routing granularity: CogRouter \\citep{cogrouter-2026} adapts per-step depth, ARES \\citep{ares-2026} selects effort levels, and MTRouter \\citep{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn settings; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models; cost-optimization routing (FrugalGPT~\\citep{frugalgpt-2023}; AutoMix~\\citep{automix-2024}) cascades queries across model tiers. These methods optimize \\emph{which} model to invoke; our framework diagnoses \\emph{what} drives the routing decision.\nConcurrently, \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing policies under sparse terminal rewards, the closest setting to ours.\nUnlike token-level adaptive computation (Mixture-of-Depths~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024}; early exit~\\citep{xin2020deebert}) and Mixture-of-Experts~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode-level granularity. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to flip labels; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes}.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in Reinforcement Learning (RL).}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent and concurrent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \\citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \\citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \\citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. The concurrent SALT~\\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}\nDeath avoidance connects to constrained Markov decision processes (MDPs; \\citealp{altman1999constrained}), risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk}, safe RL \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, and shielding \\citep{alshiekh2018safe}; Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing} optimize against worst-case outcomes; our framework instead diagnoses which signals derive from catastrophic risk. Classical methods assume known dynamics or differentiable constraints, unavailable for LLM agents. \\citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions as a failure category; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench \\citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address model selection and systematic evaluation for agent safety. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified instance of the potential outcomes framework~\\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\\citep{pearl2009causality}. \\citet{bonagiri2025check} show selective quitting yields safety gains; our framework explains \\emph{why}---death avoidance dominates routing signal.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nMethods for augmenting LLMs with tools span self-supervised invocation~\\citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, task-level decomposition~\\citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based routing~\\citep{retool-2025,tool-learning-survey-2024}. All focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?} Concurrent work examines \\emph{when} to invoke tools~\\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026,tool-overuse-illusion-2026}; our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\\% of non-tie labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing ranges from learned halting~\\citep{graves2016act} and token-level recursion~\\citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} to per-step depth~\\citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} and multi-turn settings~\\citep{mtrouter-2026}; in death-dominated environments, the task-type-level binary signal is structurally inaccessible to these step-level approaches (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}). Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards, the closest setting to ours. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and MoE~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions~\\citep{olson2021counterfactual,gajcin2023counterfactual,amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes}.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments.}\nScienceWorld~\\citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} and AgentBench~\\citep{agentbench-2024} provide complementary evaluation. ReAct~\\citep{react-2023} is our internal baseline; Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} and LATS~\\citep{lats-2024} extend it. The concurrent SALT~\\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}\nDeath avoidance connects to constrained MDPs~\\citep{altman1999constrained}, safe RL~\\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, shielding~\\citep{alshiekh2018safe}, and CVaR methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing}; classical methods assume known dynamics, unavailable for LLM agents. \\citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions; SafeRoute~\\citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench~\\citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address agent safety evaluation. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified potential outcomes framework~\\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\\citep{pearl2009causality}."
}

Model-selection routing~\\citep{routellm-2024,frugalgpt-2023,automix-2024} optimizes \\emph{which} model to invoke; \\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing under sparse rewards, the closest setting to ours. Unlike token-level adaptive computation~\\citep{mixture-of-depths-2024,xin2020deebert} and MoE~\\citep{switch-transformer-2022}, our framework diagnoses aggregate routing signal at episode granularity.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in Reinforcement Learning (RL).}",
          "-Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent and concurrent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training.",
          "+\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation.}",
          "+Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions~\\citep{olson2021counterfactual,gajcin2023counterfactual,amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents~\\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \\emph{before} training. Counterfactual data augmentation~\\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs; we execute full counterfactual \\emph{episodes}.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}",
          "-ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \\citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \\citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \\citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. The concurrent SALT~\\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.",
          "+\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments.}",
          "+ScienceWorld~\\citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} and AgentBench~\\citep{agentbench-2024} provide complementary evaluation. ReAct~\\citep{react-2023} is our internal baseline; Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} and LATS~\\citep{lats-2024} extend it. The concurrent SALT~\\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}",
          "-Death avoidance connects to constrained Markov decision processes (MDPs; \\citealp{altman1999constrained}), risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk}, safe RL \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}, and shielding \\citep{alshiekh2018safe}; Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) methods~\\citep{chow2015risk,tamar2015optimizing} optimize against worst-case outcomes; our framework instead diagnoses which signals derive from catastrophic risk. Classical methods assume known dynamics or differentiable constraints, unavailable for LLM agents. \\citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions as a failure category; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench \\citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address model selection and systematic evaluation for agent safety. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified instance of the potential outcomes framework~\\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\\citep{pearl2009causality}. \\citet{bonagiri2025check} show selective quitting yields safety gains; our framework explains \\emph{why}---death avoidance dominates routing signal.",
          "\\ No newline at end of file",

68	The labeling function is \emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \texttt{no\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.

ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}",
    "newString": "The labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Episode-Level Counterfactual Labeling}\n\\label{alg:labeling}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Task instance $t$, computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$\n\\ENSURE Label $\\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}, {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}$\n\\FOR{each $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$}\n    \\STATE Run episode $\\tau_m$ with pure-strategy $m$\n    \\STATE Record score $s_m$ and death indicator $d_m$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\IF{$d_{\\tt int} = 1$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 0$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = 0$ \\AND $d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{death asymmetry}\n\\ELSIF{$d_{\\tt int} = d_{\\tt det} = 1$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{both dead}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt det} > s_{\\tt int}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt det\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSIF{$s_{\\tt int} > s_{\\tt det}$}\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt int\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{score comparison}\n\\ELSE\n    \\STATE $\\ell \\leftarrow {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}$ \\COMMENT{tied alive}\n\\ENDIF\n\\RETURN $\\ell$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/label_decomposition.pdf}\n\\caption{Episode-level label decomposition ($N{=}245$). Of 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: 54 where death is the sole discriminator and 21 with co-occurring score differences. All 20 non-death labels originate from a single task type (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}).}\n\\label{fig:label-decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through two components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}). A supplementary Signal Concentration Index (SCI; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) measures whether signal is concentrated in a few task types. Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy (an umbrella term: ground-truth simulator access in ScienceWorld, code interpreter in MATH, admissible-action enumeration in ALFWorld---see \\S\\ref{sec:cross-env} for environment-specific operationalizations) that eliminates observation uncertainty; these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$.\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling degenerates under sparse terminal rewards: on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps (5{,}935 of 6{,}094 across 11 prescreened task types) produce identical intermediate scores, yielding no per-step routing signal. Episode-level comparison---directly measuring terminal outcomes---is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying mode $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM v0.21.0) on ScienceWorld \\citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty; complete prompt templates are provided in Appendix~\\ref{app:prompt-templates}. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Death\\% & $N$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nSingle-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\\\\nReflexion ($3{\\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\\\\nQwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$) & 30.6 & 255 \\\\\nDeterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{3}{@{}l}{\\textit{10 death-prone tasks only:}} \\\\\nDeath-prone baseline & 48.0 & 200 \\\\\nRetry ($5{\\times}$) & 43.5 & 200 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under improvement methods. Top: all 11 tasks. Bottom: 10 death-prone tasks (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded). No intervention approaches deterministic-level safety.}\n\\label{tab:agent-improvement}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). Both-dead episodes receive \\texttt{no\\_preference} because ScienceWorld's fixed $-100$ penalty makes death timing uninformative. ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011Ft4j8aV419U6ANpkVZkDb`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
8	\label{fig:decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). Taxonomically redundant tasks share identical action vocabularies, reward functions, and environmental dynamics, differing only in target entity; we retain one representative per family (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset; increasing the 30-step budget might surface signal in excluded types (see Limitations). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
12	
13	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
14	\label{sec:death-decomp}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\% CI$_{\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\S\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
21	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$); a detailed failure-mode analysis reveals that all 105 internal-strategy deaths are triggered by the \texttt{focus} action (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\to 1$ implies $r \to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory: agents execute long \texttt{connect} sequences before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers the $-100$ penalty.
22	
23	\begin{figure}[t]
24	\centering
25	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
26	\vspace{-2mm}
27	\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'').}
28	\label{fig:death-timeline}
29	\end{figure}
30	
31	\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}
32	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels, while \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\Delta = +9.58$; Appendix~\ref{app:non-death-case-study}) produces 0\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\% and DAF $\geq$100\% (Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}).
33	
34	\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}
35	Non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$ for DAF; cluster-robust CI $[73.3\%, 100.0\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}); two independent replications---cross-model (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${}\,{=}\,89.7\%$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}) and same-model with a different environment seed (seed=123, $N{=}220$, DAF${}\,{=}\,89.7\%$; Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness})---both converge within 0.7~pp. Exact permutation ($p=0.002$) and sign tests ($p<0.001$) on $K{=}11$ task-type clusters provide distribution-free confirmation (Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}; supplementary evidence in Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}).
36	
37	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}
38	\label{sec:cross-model}
39	
40	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\%$ vs.\ deterministic $11.4\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), DAF $= 88.2\%$ (95\% CI: $[80.2\%, 95.1\%]$; $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +33.17$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +3.9$), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.
41	Both models show ${\sim}42\%$ internal vulnerability ($+26.5$ vs.\ $+30.0$~pp differential; per-task details in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
42	A third model family, Gemma-2-9B-it~\citep{gemma2-2024} (sliding window attention, vs.\ full attention in Qwen/Llama), independently confirms the pattern: DAF${=}89.7\%$ (95\% CI $[79.5\%, 98.4\%]$; $N{=}220$), with all 10 death-prone task types showing positive $\Delta$DR (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models thus converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\times}$ scaling to two independent 70B-class models (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms persistence---and intensification---across capability levels (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\%$).
43	
44	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
45	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
46	
47	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$ baseline, $\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}\footnote{Reflexion uses a fixed allocation of 20 episodes per task type ($20 \times 11 = 220$), vs.\ the baseline's variable 20--40 per type ($N{=}245$).} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters; both strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\%$ death rate ($\Delta\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$; 7B: $90.4\%$), with 5 internal-preferred episodes (vs.\ 9 at 7B; $2.0\%$ vs.\ $3.7\%$).
48	A second 70B-class model, Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct ($N{=}220$), intensifies the pattern: internal death rate $48.6\%$, deterministic $20.0\%$ ($\Delta\text{DR} = +28.6$~pp), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.30$, yielding DAF $= 101.0\%$ (95\% CI: $[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$). DAF exceeding 100\% indicates that death avoidance more than fully explains the deterministic advantage---when both agents survive, the internal agent performs comparably or slightly better. Label fraction rises to $91.3\%$ (67 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
49	Two independent 70B-class models thus confirm that $10{\times}$ scaling does not attenuate death dominance; it intensifies proportional dominance because the deterministic strategy maintains substantially lower death rates than the internal strategy at both 70B scales ($2.75$--$20.0\%$ vs.\ $30.6$--$48.6\%$).
50	
51	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
52	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
53	
54	\begin{figure}[t]
55	\centering
56	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}
57	\caption{Death rate vs.\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\% CIs.}
58	\label{fig:dose-response}
59	\end{figure}
60	
61	\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}
62	\label{sec:math-contrast}
63	\label{sec:cross-env}
64	
65	We examine MATH and ALFWorld as boundary probes (not cross-domain validation). The deterministic oracle differs in information privilege across environments---ground-truth simulator (ScienceWorld), code interpreter (MATH), admissible-action enumeration (ALFWorld; \S\ref{sec:problem-setup})---so cross-environment results validate the diagnostic procedure's reusability, not the transferability of specific findings.
66	
67	\paragraph{MATH.} On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$, code interpreter), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, McNemar $p = 0.81$), with 84.4\% no-preference. Without catastrophic failure, no routing signal emerges.
68	
69	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} (6 task types, 135 games) provides the deterministic strategy with admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch, with no death mechanism ($\delta = 0$, DAF $= 0\%$). With Qwen2.5-7B, 35 of 37 non-tie labels (94.6\%) favor the information-advantaged strategy (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-env-details}); Llama-3.1-8B (135 games) yields 16 of 16 non-tie labels in the same direction (100\%). Llama shows fewer non-tie episodes (11.9\% vs.\ 25.9\%) but perfect directional consistency, confirming that information asymmetry, not death avoidance, drives routing signal in ALFWorld regardless of model family. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism (DAF $= 0\%$ vs.\ $90.4\%$) without modification. Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal} summarizes label distributions across all three environments.
70	
71	\begin{figure}[t]
72	\centering
73	\includegraphics[width=0.95\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
74	\vspace{-2mm}
75	\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}
76	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
77	\end{figure}
78	
79	\subsection{Baselines}
80	\label{sec:baselines}
81	
82	\begin{table}[t]
83	\centering
84	\small
85	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
86	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
87	\toprule
88	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
89	\midrule
90	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
91	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
92	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
93	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
94	\midrule
95	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}}
98	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}
99	\label{tab:baselines}
100	\end{table}
101	
102	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall). Even with perfect per-task death-risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform the always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).
103	
104	\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}
105	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
106	
107	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\sim}$88\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\%--800\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\% minority class).
108

  "old_string": "We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). Taxonomically redundant tasks share identical action vocabularies, reward functions, and environmental dynamics, differing only in target entity; we retain one representative per family (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset; increasing the 30-step budget might surface signal in excluded types (see Limitations). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\\Delta\\text{DR} = +38.2$\\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\\overline{\\Delta\\text{DR}} = +32.0$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.",
  "new_string": "We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\\overline{\\Delta\\text{DR}} = +38.2$\\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task})."
}

  "old_string": "Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, 95\\% CI$_{\\text{nd}}$: $[0.34, 5.57]$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\to 0$, DAF $\\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\\geq}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.",
  "new_string": "DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\\S\\ref{sec:daf})."
}

          "+DAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}",
          " The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$); a detailed failure-mode analysis reveals that all 105 internal-strategy deaths are triggered by the \\texttt{focus} action (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\\to 1$ implies $r \\to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory: agents execute long \\texttt{connect} sequences before a terminal \\texttt{focus on} triggers the $-100$ penalty."
        ]
      }
    ],

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}\nThe Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels, while \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\\Delta = +9.58$; Appendix~\\ref{app:non-death-case-study}) produces 0\\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\\% and DAF $\\geq$100\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}).\n\n\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\nNon-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$ for DAF; cluster-robust CI $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}); two independent replications---cross-model (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${}\\,{=}\\,89.7\\%$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) and same-model with a different environment seed (seed=123, $N{=}220$, DAF${}\\,{=}\\,89.7\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness})---both converge within 0.7~pp. Exact permutation ($p=0.002$) and sign tests ($p<0.001$) on $K{=}11$ task-type clusters provide distribution-free confirmation (Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}; supplementary evidence in Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}\nAll 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels; only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\\%$) converge within 0.7~pp."
}
