<details open><summary>🔧 Tool call — Edit · `toolu_013tjS9GseARwDCJPTVMBgky`</summary>

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$ attempts without self-correction) yields marginal reduction on death-prone tasks ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$ baseline, $\\Delta = -4.5$~pp). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023}\\footnote{Reflexion uses a fixed allocation of 20 episodes per task type ($20 \\times 11 = 220$), vs.\\ the baseline's variable 20--40 per type ($N{=}245$).} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\\times}$ parameters; both strategies independently collected at 72B, 255 episodes across all 11 task types) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$. The 72B deterministic strategy achieves $2.75\\%$ death rate ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +27.8$~pp), and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.28$, yielding DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$; 7B: $90.4\\%$), with 5 internal-preferred episodes (vs.\\ 9 at 7B; $2.0\\%$ vs.\\ $3.7\\%$).\nA second 70B-class model, Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct ($N{=}220$), intensifies the pattern: internal death rate $48.6\\%$, deterministic $20.0\\%$ ($\\Delta\\text{DR} = +28.6$~pp), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.30$, yielding DAF $= 101.0\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$). DAF exceeding 100\\% indicates that death avoidance more than fully explains the deterministic advantage---when both agents survive, the internal agent performs comparably or slightly better. Label fraction rises to $91.3\\%$ (67 det-preferred, 2 int-preferred; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}).\nTwo independent 70B-class models thus confirm that $10{\\times}$ scaling does not attenuate death dominance; it intensifies proportional dominance because the deterministic strategy maintains substantially lower death rates than the internal strategy at both 70B scales ($2.75$--$20.0\\%$ vs.\\ $30.6$--$48.6\\%$).",
  "new_string": "We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance."
}
```

Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}",
          " We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response})."

Even with perfect per-task death-risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform the always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).",
    "newString": "Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}). The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.75\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\\to 1 \\Rightarrow r \\to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}\n\\label{fig:decomposition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\\overline{\\Delta\\text{DR}} = +38.2$\\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\\,pp; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task}).\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}\n\\label{sec:death-decomp}\n\nOf the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\\%; 95\\% CI: $[70.7\\%, 87.0\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}).\n\nDAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic; all 105 internal deaths are triggered by the \\texttt{focus on} action (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task $\\Delta$DR correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ at $r = 0.97$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}), a structural consequence of DAF$\\to 1$ rather than an independent finding. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \\texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \\texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\\to$ fatal focus'').}\n\\label{fig:death-timeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}\nAll 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels; only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nLlama-3.1-8B~\\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\\%$ and $89.7\\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models converge on DAF $88$--$90\\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\\%$).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate vs.\\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe probe two environments as boundary cases (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy; Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall). Even with perfect per-task death-risk knowledge, threshold routing cannot outperform the always-deterministic baseline (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\\%--800\\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\\% minority class).\n",

ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}). The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. An implicit router (Qwen3, Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA); Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline.\\footnote{The near-chance performance is consistent with noisy-label learning bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative, class imbalance is extreme (90.5\\% deterministic-preferred, 3.7\\% minority), and paraphrase-based text augmentation (25\\%--800\\%) provides no improvement (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrades to chance (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}), as does a task-type-only classifier ($64.7\\%$ in-distribution, $50.0\\%$ leave-one-task-out (LOTO)), confirming routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$ (95\\% CI: $[-16.33, -5.27]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Results remain inconclusive under the current data regime ($N{=}245$, 3.7\\% minority class).\n",

80	\IF{$d_{\tt int} = 1$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 0$}
81	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
82	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = 0$ \AND $d_{\tt det} = 1$}
83	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{death asymmetry}
84	\ELSIF{$d_{\tt int} = d_{\tt det} = 1$}
85	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{both dead}
86	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt det} > s_{\tt int}$}
87	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt det\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
88	\ELSIF{$s_{\tt int} > s_{\tt det}$}
89	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt int\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{score comparison}
90	\ELSE
91	    \STATE $\ell \leftarrow {\tt no\text{-}pref}$ \COMMENT{tied alive}
92	\ENDIF
93	\RETURN $\ell$
94	\end{algorithmic}
95	\end{algorithm}
96	
97	\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}
98	\label{sec:daf}
99	
100	To quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\delta = \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt int}] - \mathbb{E}[d^{\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$, where $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} \ll P$ (in our data, $\bar{s}_{\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\approx}\,\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:
101	\begin{equation}
102	    \Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \underbrace{\delta \cdot P}_{\text{death contribution}} + \Delta_{\text{nd}}
103	\end{equation}
104	The approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\%$ of $\Delta_{\text{full}}$).
105	
106	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
107	\begin{equation}
108	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
109	\end{equation}%
110	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
111	
112	Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}).
113	
114	\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}
115	DAF (90.4\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \gg$ typical non-death $\Delta{\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.
116	
117	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
118	We quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}.
119	
120	We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}), reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
121	
122	\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}
123	At step level, 97.4\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\S\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
124	
```

    "oldString": "Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}\nDAF (90.4\\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \\gg$ typical non-death $\\Delta{\\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. ```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\begin{figure}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
7	\caption{Per-task death rate differential vs.\ performance gap ($n{=}11$ task types). The near-perfect correlation ($r = 0.97$) is a structural consequence of death dominance (DAF $\to 1 \Rightarrow r \to 1$), not an independent finding. The sole outlier (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, red diamond) has zero deaths; its $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ arises entirely from non-death score differences.}
8	\label{fig:decomposition}
9	\end{figure}
10	
11	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +38.2$\,pp $>$ included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
12	
13	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
14	\label{sec:death-decomp}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels (Figure~\ref{fig:label-decomposition}), 75 (78.9\%; 95\% CI: $[70.7\%, 87.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\% CI$_{\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\S\ref{sec:daf}).
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task structure.}
```

Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}).\n\nDAF $= 90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\\% CI$_{\\text{DAF}}$: $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap). The 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-invariant characterization (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}).\n\n\\paragraph{Per-task structure.}\nThe internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic; all 105 internal deaths are triggered by the \\texttt{focus on} action (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). Per-task $\\Delta$DR correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ at $r = 0.97$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}), a structural consequence of DAF$\\to 1$ rather than an independent finding. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} illustrates the typical death trajectory.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \\texttt{focus on} (arrow) appears in final steps after long \\texttt{connect} sequences (``connect loop $\\to$ fatal focus'').}\n\\label{fig:death-timeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Binary structure and robustness.}\nAll 10 death-prone task types produce 100\\% death-asymmetry labels; only \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Bootstrap 95\\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\\%, 99.1\\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\\%, 100.0\\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}\n\\label{sec:cross-model}\n\nLlama-3.1-8B~\\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\\%$ and $89.7\\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models converge on DAF $88$--$90\\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (\\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\\%$).\n\n\\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}\n\\label{sec:agent-improvement}\n\nWe test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\\%$ vs.\\ $48.0\\%$). Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[86.1\\%, 97.9\\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\\%$ ($[95.3\\%, 107.5\\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}\nWe extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\\% \\to 43.5\\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\\%$ for $N \\geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\\sim}8$~pp at 80\\% power; Appendix~\\ref{app:dose-response}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{Death rate vs.\\ trial count for Retry and Reflexion on 10 death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$). Reflexion drops steeply through $N{=}3$ then plateaus; Retry is nearly flat. Horizontal lines: 72B ($30.6\\%$) and deterministic ($16.3\\%$) baselines. Error bands: Wilson 95\\% CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dose-response}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Environment Boundary Probes}\n\\label{sec:math-contrast}\n\\label{sec:cross-env}\n\nWe probe two environments as boundary cases (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal-reversal}). On MATH Level~4--5~\\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$), internal and code-interpreter accuracy are near-identical ($73.5\\%$ vs.\\ $73.0\\%$, $p = 0.81$): no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ALFWorld~\\citep{alfworld-2021} (135 games, no death mechanism, DAF $= 0\\%$) shows 94.6\\% of non-tie labels favoring the information-advantaged strategy; Llama-3.1-8B replicates at 100\\%. The framework diagnoses this qualitatively different mechanism without modification.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.95\\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both favor deterministic via different mechanisms (death avoidance vs.\\ information asymmetry); MATH shows no routing signal.}\n\\label{fig:signal-reversal}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nStrategy & Score & 95\\% CI & Death \\% \\\\\n\\midrule\nAlways-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\\\\nAlways-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\\\\nRandom & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\\\\nOracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained Router$^\\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes). $\\dagger$\\,Single seed (42); see Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\end{table}\n\nAlways-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points (Table~\\ref{tab:baselines}). The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \\emph{oracle degeneracy} (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}).\n\n\\subsection{Router Training: Inconclusive Results}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nA Qwen3 LoRA router (0.6B--4B; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained on counterfactual labels shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$); episode-level evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below majority-vote.\\footnote{Consistent with noisy-label bounds~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: ${\\sim}$88\\% of step-level labels are uninformative with 90.5\\%/3.7\\% class imbalance (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).} Leave-one-task-out degrades to chance ($49.2\\%$), confirming signal is a per-task-type constant. Deployed end-to-end, the best router scores $-10.70$, not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$).\n",

```text
60	All 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels; only \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths) produces non-death signal (SCI $= 0.21$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}). Bootstrap 95\% CIs: DAF $[81.3\%, 99.1\%]$; cluster-robust $[73.3\%, 100.0\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Two independent replications (Gemma-2-9B-it, DAF${=}89.7\%$; seed=123, DAF${=}89.7\%$) converge within 0.7~pp.
61	
62	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}
63	\label{sec:cross-model}
64	
65	Llama-3.1-8B~\citep{llama3-2024} ($N{=}220$) and Gemma-2-9B-it~\citep{gemma2-2024} ($N{=}220$) independently replicate death dominance: DAF $= 88.2\%$ and $89.7\%$ respectively, with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\times}$ scaling to two 70B-class models (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) intensifies the pattern (DAF${=}92.4$--$101.0\%$).
66	
67	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
68	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
69	
70	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry ($N{=}5$) yields marginal reduction ($43.5\%$ vs.\ $48.0\%$). Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death to $31.4\%$ but the gap to deterministic remains $15.1$~pp; under matched-episode analysis ($N{=}3$), $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF${}>100\%$. Scaling to Qwen2.5-72B ($N{=}255$) reduces death rate to $30.6\%$ with DAF $= 92.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[86.1\%, 97.9\%]$). A second 70B model, Llama-3.1-70B ($N{=}220$), yields DAF $= 101.0\%$ ($[95.3\%, 107.5\%]$): death avoidance more than fully accounts for the gap. Two 70B-class models confirm that scaling intensifies rather than attenuates death dominance.
71	
72	\paragraph{Dose-response analysis.}
73	We extend both interventions to $N{=}5$ trials on death-prone tasks ($n{=}200$; Figure~\ref{fig:dose-response}). Retry is nearly flat ($48.0\% \to 43.5\%$). Reflexion drops steeply to $34.0\%$ at $N{=}3$ ($\Delta = -13.5$~pp, $p = 0.003$) then stabilizes ($34$--$36.5\%$ for $N \geq 3$, pairwise $p > 0.5$); both the decline and plateau are well-powered (MDE ${\sim}8$~pp at 80\% power; Appendix~\ref{app:dose-response}).
74

126	\label{tab:per-task}

129	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
130	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).

120	find-non-living-thing$^\dagger$ & 40 & $+9.58$ & 0  & 0    & 16 / 4 / 20 \\
121	\midrule
122	\textbf{All (11 tasks)} & \textbf{245} & $\mathbf{+29.60}$ & \textbf{42.9} & \textbf{16.3} & \textbf{86 / 9 / 150} \\
123	\bottomrule
124	\end{tabular}
125	\caption{Per-task decomposition of the death avoidance effect. D\%: death rate for internal (int) and deterministic (det) strategies. D/I/N: episode labels (det-preferred / internal-preferred / no-preference). Sorted by $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ descending. The \textit{All} row is an episode-weighted aggregate across 245 episodes. $\dagger$\,Only task with zero deaths under both strategies; $n{=}40$ because data was collected in two batches, with the second batch adding 20 episodes to increase statistical power for this zero-death outlier.}
126	\label{tab:per-task}
127	\end{table}
128	
129	\paragraph{Task selection and anti-cherry-picking.}
130	Eighteen of the 19 excluded task types (95\%) exhibit death-asymmetric patterns (Table~\ref{tab:excluded-death}): internal-reasoning death rates range from 40\% to 60\% (mean 53.9\%), while deterministic death rates range from 0\% to 40\% (mean 15.8\%), yielding a mean death-rate differential of $+38.2$\,pp, exceeding the $+28.7$\,pp for included tasks. The included subset is thus \emph{less} death-asymmetric than the excluded subset, the opposite of what a cherry-picking hypothesis would predict. The sole exception is \texttt{grow-plant}, an intrinsically safe task with zero deaths under both strategies, paralleling \texttt{find-non-living-thing} in the included set. The 19 excluded tasks fall into three independently motivated categories: five used during pipeline development (standard hold-out practice), seven taxonomically redundant with included family representatives (deduplication), and seven where the 7B backbone achieves near-zero task progress regardless of strategy (agent capability floor). In no case was a task excluded because death dominance was absent. Exclusion is driven by absence of non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 0$ for 53\% of excluded tasks) and standard deduplication practices. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} serves as an internal counterexample: zero deaths under both strategies yet included because it exhibits non-death routing signal ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +9.58$ entirely from score differences), and deterministic is not always preferred (4 of 20 non-tie labels favor internal).
```

556	\caption{Cross-model and cross-environment replication summary. \textbf{ScienceWorld}: death dominance replicates across three model families in the 7--9B range and intensifies under $10{\times}$ scaling (two 70B-class models, DAF $92.4$--$101.0\%$); DAF 95\% CIs from non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$). \textbf{ALFWorld}: DAF $= 0\%$ (no death mechanism); routing signal arises from information asymmetry. \textbf{MATH}: no routing signal without catastrophic failure. Signal column: diagnosed routing mechanism. DR = death rate. Gemma-2's wider CI reflects one zero-death task type (\texttt{find-non-living-thing}); excluding it yields DAF $= 99.9\%$ $[99.7, 100.0]$.}
557	\label{tab:cross-model-env}
558	\end{table}
559	
560	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
561	\label{app:failure-modes}
562	
563	All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, and applies it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer and triggers the $-100$ death penalty. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40 for deterministic) reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.
564	
565	Manual inspection identifies four failure categories: (A) no task awareness, where the agent executes \texttt{focus on} without reading the task; (B) task-aware but lacking action grounding, where the agent correctly identifies the objective but cannot map it to valid action primitives; (D) genuine attempts with execution failure; and (E) initial progress wasted by early errors. Category B dominates: agents demonstrate task comprehension but systematically fail to translate it into correct ScienceWorld actions. This taxonomy was produced by a single annotator; no inter-annotator agreement was computed. The categories are intended as qualitative illustrations, not as formal labels with reliability guarantees.
566	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
567	
568	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
569	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
570	
571	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
572	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy; both are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions, with \texttt{focus on} moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$). Crucially, Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} reveals that within death episodes, internal and deterministic strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$). The survival gap (105 vs.\ 40 deaths) therefore reflects how often each strategy enters catastrophic states, not how it behaves within those states. Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps.
573	
574	\begin{figure}[h]
575	\centering
576	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}
577	\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}105$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}140$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is moderately more frequent in death episodes ($5.4\%$ vs.\ $3.4\%$), while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
578	\label{fig:death-action-dist}
579	\end{figure}
580	
581	% Figure moved to main text (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp})
582	
583	\begin{figure}[h]
584	\centering
585	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}
586	\caption{Non-\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. Within death episodes, both strategies exhibit comparable \texttt{focus on} rates (${\sim}5\%$); the internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different action selection within those states.}
587	\label{fig:death-comparison}
588	\end{figure}
